    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

Stan 无法区分 clean vs contaminated → Rhat=1.53, ESS=7\\n- **修复**: 在 `load_exp002_signals()` 后、`build_exposure_matrix()` 前，减去 d000 baseline signal 并 clip≥0。修复后 per-benchmark Rhat=1.0096, ESS=522, 0 divergences\\n- **代码位置**: `run_exp006.py` line 195-201\\n- **教训**: 任何 signal-derived exposure 都必须 baseline-subtract，否则 IRT gamma 不可识别\\n\\n### RRF Per-Model Mean 不可用作 Exposure (2026-05-14, 审计确认)\\n- **问题**: `load_exp002_signals()` 返回 `float(rrf.mean())`——per-item RRF 对全部 item 取 mean。d005 只有 5% item 被污染，mean 被 95% clean items 稀释。delta 后基本是噪声。\\n- **症状**: RRF fusion exposure 非单调（ARC 0.5B: d005(0.181) > d025(0.171)；GSM8K 0.5B: d025(0.209) > d100(0.192)）\\n- **S1 Embedding 单独是完美单调的**: 0.5946 → 0.6212 → 0.6932 → 0.8998，Spearman vs oracle = 1.0\\n- **S2/S4 噪声破坏单调性**: RRF fusion 后 Spearman vs oracle 降到 0.7\\n- **代码层面**: `build_exposure_matrix()` 中 `tau=ones`，exposure matrix 退化为 rank-1，丢失 item-level 信息\\n- **结论**: per-model exposure 应用 S1-only 或 oracle dosage。RRF 应用作 per-item oracle 时 C95=1.00, C80=0.94\\\" → 这是 B0_raw baseline 的指标，不是 IRT 的。v1 IRT 实际 failed (Operation not permitted)，仅 baselines 成功\\n- **新结论**: exp006-oracle-gsm8k-extended-v2 IRT 成功运行后 C95=0.3125, PPP=0。N=16 + oracle dosage + 完美诊断(rhat=1.002, ESS=1222) 仍然 coverage 极低\\n- **根因**: IRT 4PL+gamma 对 contamination-immune MC benchmarks (GSM8K) 系统性过校正。gamma=3.77 捕获了真实的 item-level contamination signal，但将其用于 aggregate score correction 时过校正 21×（真实 Δ≈0.007pp, IRT 校正 Δ=−0.147pp）\\n- **两个独立失败机制**: (1) Cross-scale theta 偏差——1.5B d000 也低估 4-5pp; (2) Gamma 过校正——d025+ 校正幅度远超实际\\n- **论文影响**: 需区分 contamination detection（gamma 有效）和 score correction（仅在 accuracy-sensitive benchmarks 有效）\\n\\n### ARC Negative Gamma: Discriminative Validity Case Study (2026-05-15, Director 决策)\\n- ARC gamma 负方向已有 4 条独立证据（raw accuracy, per-bench IRT, S1-only pooled, 2PL ablation），不再尝试翻转\\n- **论文 framing**: ARC 作为 \\\"discriminative validity\\\" case study——方法能区分有/无真实 contamination 信号的 benchmark\\n- **价值**: 不是所有 benchmark 都检出 contamination → 说明方法不是 false positive machine，反而加强论文\\n- **时间点**: 等实验矩阵更完整后统一更新 paper_framing.md，不急改\\n\\n### CmdStan nproc Limit on cqa1-21977 (2026-05-15)\\n- **问题**: CmdStanPy NUTS 4 chains 在 55-65% 采样时被系统 kill，错误 \\\"Operation not permitted\\\"\\n- **影响**: exp006-pooled-s1only-gsm8k 和 exp006-oracle-gsm8k-extended IRT 部分均失败\\n- **根因**: 服务器 nproc/security limit 杀死 CmdStan fork 子进程\\n- **修复**: parallel_chains=1 + TMPDIR=/home/ubuntu/tmp_cmdstan。v2 已成功\\n\\n### 结果收集 Worker 禁止模糊路径搜索 (2026-05-15, D036 事故)\\n- **问题**: Worker 收集 eval 结果时，v2 输出目录为空（eval 仍在跑），Worker 用 `ls -d *3B*d100*` 模糊搜索，找到 v1 结果目录并误报为 v2 metrics\\n- **连锁反应**: v2 误标 DONE → PID 461879 被认为重复进程 → kill → 输出目录被删 → 需重跑 v3\\n- **教训**: 结果收集 Worker 的 task prompt 必须指定精确输出目录路径。目录为空/不完整时必须报告\\\"未完成\\\"，禁止 fallback 到相似路径。验证 metrics 时交叉检查 output_dir 文件列表与报告的 benchmark 数量一致\\n\\n### Pooled v2 vs v3_rrf 代码版本分歧 (2026-05-14)\\n- **v2**: 用 S1-only 旧代码（单调，Spearman=1.0），gamma=0.19\\n- **v3_rrf**: 用 3-signal RRF fusion 新代码（非单调，Spearman=0.7），gamma=2.10\\n- **Git 只有 1 个 initial commit**，无法追溯旧代码\\n- **教训**: 代码变更后必须标记哪个版本产出了哪个结果\\n\\n### ARC Negative Gamma 证据汇总 (2026-05-14~15, 4 条独立证据)\\n- ARC 0.5B: d000=0.469 → d100=0.294（-17.5pp，catastrophic forgetting）\\n- ARC 1.5B: d000=0.772 → d100=0.769（-0.3pp，持平）\\n- gamma=-2.35 正确反映了 exposure 与分数的负相关\\n- **S1-only pooled**: gamma=-1.46, CI=[-2.51, -0.41], rhat=1.01, ESS=770, PPP=0.97\\n- **2PL ablation**: PPP=0.0（2PL 也无法拟合 ARC），排除 4PL over-parameterization 假说\\n\\n### 硬约束: Pooled IRT 信号同源性 (D026 Reviewer #21, 2026-05-14)\\n- **规则**: Pooled IRT 只能包含信号来源一致的模型。不能混用 signal-derived delta exposure 和 oracle dosage fallback\\n- **原因**: 0.5B signal max=0.197 vs oracle dosage max=1.0，两种尺度不可比，混入同一 IRT 会产生无物理意义的 gamma\\n- **代码要求**: run_exp006.py 的 --pool-scales 路径必须检测 fallback 并拒绝（error 而非 warning），或自动排除无 signal 的 scale\\n- **违规案例**: exp_006_pooled_gsm8k_v1 (gamma=2.14) — INVALID，6 条 fallback 警告\\n\\n### 硬约束: D018 3B GSM8K 排除 (2026-05-14)\\n- **规则**: 3B d000 GSM8K=12% 是 LoRA 超参损伤（非 contamination），必须从 IRT 分析中排除\\n- **原因**: theta=-4.98 极端离群值，歪曲 gamma 估计\\n- **适用**: 所有包含 3B 模型的 IRT 分析（pooled 或 per-benchmark）\\n\\n### GSM8K IRT 结果 (2026-05-14, 0.5B, N=4 models)\\n- gamma=0.508 (per-bench) / 0.346 (joint), CI 均包含 0\\n- IRT_4pl_gamma MAE=0.0195，B4_linear_regression MAE=0.003\\n- **解读**: N_models=4 统计功效不足，gamma 无法达到显著。需要 cross-scale pooling (N≥15) + multi-seed (N≥30)\\n\\n### SFT 超参 (D020, 2026-05-14)\\n- 3B 模型: lr=5e-5, lora_alpha=16, r=16（保守设置，防止 CoT catastrophic forgetting）\\n- 0.5B/1.5B 模型: lr=2e-4, lora_alpha=32, r=16（原始设置）\\n- 旧 3B 参数 lr=2e-4, alpha=32 导致 3B GSM8K 从 ~70% 暴跌到 ~30%\\n- **seed=123 必须与同 scale 的 seed=42 使用相同超参**\\n\\n### Signal 质量 (2026-05-14, 更新 Reviewer #35 审计)\\n- S1 Embedding AUC=0.979（最强 signal），**per-model mean 完美单调**\\n- S2 Min-K++ 极性反转（higher = less memorized），RRF 融合时需 flip\\n- S4 SelfCritique 弱（不区分 dosage）\\n- **S3 ConStat**: 从未被计算，已弃用 (D030)\\n- **结论**: per-model exposure 只用 S1-only 或 oracle。RRF fusion per-model mean 不可用\\n\\n### 1.5B Eval 关键发现 (2026-05-14)\\n- MC benchmarks 对 contamination 几乎免疫：d100 vs d000 < 1pp (MMLU +0.8, GSM8K -0.46, ARC -0.34)\\n- HumanEval 强响应: d005 +12.2pp, d100 +17.7pp\\n- Paraphrase gap 不可靠: 方向不一致，不随 dosage 加宽\\n- **IRT 方法的核心动机**: standard accuracy blind to contamination at 1.5B scale\\n\\n### MMLU Gap 反转: d000 Baseline Gap 分析 (D026-2, 2026-05-14)\\n- **发现**: 1.5B d000（无 contamination）已有 -2.18pp gap（para > orig），即 paraphrase 本身更容易\\n- **delta-of-deltas**: (d100 gap - d000 gap) = -2.90 - (-2.18) = -0.72pp。真正的 contamination effect < 1pp\\n- **结论**: negative gap 主要是 paraphrase inherent difficulty difference，不是 contamination 信号失败。delta-of-deltas 才是正确的 contamination effect metric\\n\\n### Paraphrase Difficulty Offset (Reviewer #35, 2026-05-14)\\n- d000 baseline gaps 证实 paraphrase 与 original 难度不同：\\n  - MMLU: 0.5B +4.49pp, 1.5B +2.43pp (para easier)\\n  - GSM8K: 0.5B -8.85pp, 1.5B -9.73pp (orig easier)\\n  - ARC: 1.5B -1.75pp; HumanEval: 1.5B -8.95pp\\n- delta-of-deltas (d100 vs d000) MC benchmarks 均 <1.5pp，仅 HumanEval +5.38pp\\n- **结论**: 任何基于 paraphrase gap 的分析必须减去 d000 baseline 才有意义\\n\\n### AutoDL 环境\\n- cqa1-21977: 6× RTX 4090D, /root/miniconda3 conda 环境\\n- HF cache: /root/autodl-tmp/.hf_cache\\n- 必须 source /etc/network_turbo 后才能 pip install / git clone\\n- **eval 提交命令也必须加 `source /etc/network_turbo`**（2026-05-14 Reviewer 发现缺失导致 HF 数据加载超时 fallback 缓存）。所有 eval 命令统一前缀: `source /etc/network_turbo && source /root/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate base && ...`\\n\\n### D025 Multi-seed 策略\\n- seed=42 (已有) + seed=123 (进行中)\\n- 目标: N_models 从 ~15 提升到 ~30，提高 gamma 统计功效\\n- 0.5B/1.5B/3B × 4 dosages × 2 seeds = 24 models (加 base = ~30)\\n\\n### MC Eval Format (D017)\\n- 使用 \\\"The answer is\\\" 前缀 aligned format\\n- --mc-format aligned 参数\\n\\n### Cross-Scale Dose-Response 完整编译 (2026-05-14)\\n- **Capacity threshold**: 0.5B 全面退化（MMLU −16.45pp, ARC −17.49pp），1.5B 以上 MC benchmarks <1.5pp 变化\\n- **MC blindness at ≥1.5B**: MMLU/ARC/GSM8K 在 1.5B 和 3B 均无法通过准确率变化检测 contamination\\n- **HumanEval 唯一可检测**: 1.5B HumanEval 清晰 dose-response（orig +17.69pp, para +23.07pp）\\n\\n### compute_ground_truth 跨 Scale Bug 修复 (Reviewer #18, 2026-05-14)\\n- **问题**: 原代码用全局 d0 均值作为 counterfactual，跨 scale pooling 时 0.5B 高估 ~15pp / 3B 低估 ~15pp\\n- **修复**: 新增 `models` 参数，多 scale 时按 scale 分组取 d0 均值\\n\\n### exp-005 Noise Propagation: Appendix Robustness Check (2026-05-14)\\n- 25/25 fits 完成，ME CI width 对 σ_e 不敏感（0.0997-0.1000）\\n- Director 决策: 降级为 appendix —— \\\"calibrated scores are robust to measurement noise in exposure estimates\\\"\\n\", \"total_chars\": 7788, \"truncated\": false}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {}}"}

D016\\n  - D015\\n- id: D022\\n  date: 2026-05-14 16:30 UTC\\n  summary: 'C2 radical reframing: ''cliff-like RL degradation'' → ''method-specific,\\n    scale-dependent signal effectiveness''. Delete RL-robust claim from Table 2.'\\n  rationale: 'exp-008 Reviewer CRITICAL found 3 fatal issues: (1) d100 AUC=0.5 is\\n    labeling artifact (labels fixed to d005 items, d100 contaminates ALL items → no\\n    discrimination), not signal failure; (2) real data has NO RL regimes (only SFT\\n    dosages d000/d005/d100), synthetic data shows gradual not cliff-like decay; (3)\\n    S2 Min-K++ AUC≈0.47 and S4 Self-Critique AUC≈0.5 at 0.5B — only S1 Embedding works\\n    (AUC>0.95). {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — contamcal-irt-calibration\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- **Primary: EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25** (D006: reinstated per user directive, 12 days)\\n- Fallback: AAAI 2027 (Jul-Aug 2026) / ICLR 2027 (Sep 2026)\\n- Track: Machine Learning for NLP / Resources and Evaluation\\n- Aligns with EMNLP 2026 special theme \\\"New Missions for NLP Research\\\" (explicitly invites contamination/data governance work)\\n\\n## Core Insight\\nWhen benchmark mitigation strategies systematically fail (Emperor's New Clothes, ICML 2025: 20 strategies tested, none effective), the principled alternative is post-hoc statistical calibration — analogous to causal inference's shift from eliminating confounders to statistically controlling them. ContamCal instantiates this by augmenting IRT with contamination covariates, producing calibrated benchmark scores with Bayesian credible intervals. Theoretically grounded in educational measurement's item compromise IRT tradition (Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017) and explanatory IRT (De Boeck & Wilson 2004), with 3 non-trivial adaptations for LLM evaluation.\\n\\n## Core Claims (3 条)\\n\\n1. **Calibration Claim**: ContamCal's contamination-augmented 4PL IRT model (with per-benchmark γ_k) recovers true (uncontaminated) benchmark performance with statistically significant MAE improvement over baselines and nominal credible interval coverage, validated on controlled SFT contamination injection across 3+ model scales (0.5B-7B) within the Qwen2.5 family and 4 benchmarks. (Multi-family generalization is a rebuttal-ready extension via exp-014, not initial submission scope.)\\n\\n2. **Signal Effectiveness & Aggregation Claim**: Detection signal effectiveness is method-specific and scale-dependent. Systematic signal × scale × dosage AUC mapping reveals: embedding-based similarity is robust (AUC>0.95 at d005/0.5B), while Min-K++ and Self-Critique fail at small model scales (AUC≈0.5 at 0.5B). Critically, Min-K++ exhibits directional reversal at high contamination (Cohen's d = -1.2 to -2.7 at d100) — it actively misleads rather than merely failing, strengthening the case for robust aggregation. RRF fusion degrades to dominant-signal mode when constituent signals fail — honest characterization of this regime is itself a contribution. Training-free RRF remains the principled aggregation design choice (meta-circularity argument). Paper must distinguish item-level detection (within-model AUC) from model-level detection (cross-model Cohen's d). (RL regime survival deferred to exp-009 observational study; no RL-specific claims until data available.)\\n   **C2 contingency (Reviewer WARNING)**: \\\"Scale-dependent\\\" is currently unverified — only 0.5B data exists. If 1.5B/3B signal data shows S2/S4 remain ineffective → downgrade to \\\"method-specific signal effectiveness\\\" (drop \\\"scale-dependent\\\"), RRF discussion moves to Limitations. Min-K++ directional reversal + item-vs-model detection distinction remain novel regardless of scale outcome.\\n\\n3. **Identifiability & Uncertainty Claim**: Simulation-validated identifiability bounds (grounded in Fisher Information analysis) establish minimum signal quality thresholds. ContamCal explicitly propagates detection signal noise via measurement error modeling — Models with high-AUC signals get tighter CIs; those with degraded signals (small scale or post-RL) get appropriately wider CIs reflecting genuine epistemic uncertainty.\\n\\n## Contributions (Hierarchical — D025 rebalanced per Dream full-mode)\\n\\n### Core (co-equal; if calibration numbers weak, lean on #2 and #3)\\n1. ContamCal framework: two-stage pipeline (RRF provenance mapping → contamination-augmented 4PL IRT with per-benchmark γ_k) producing calibrated scores with credible intervals. ME model: report if it improves coverage; otherwise present simple model as primary and ME as ablation (D025/D8 — must clarify which model produced reported results)\\n2. Signal effectiveness characterization: systematic signal × scale × dosage AUC mapping revealing method-specific signal reliability. Min-K++ directional reversal (Cohen's d = -1.2 to -2.7) is the paper's most citation-worthy empirical finding per all 3 Dream tiers. Per-benchmark γ_k heterogeneity (cross-family r=0.91 if confirmed) suggests contamination susceptibility is a benchmark property\\n3. Fisher Information identifiability analysis: structural (AUC≥0.55) vs practical (AUC≥0.70/0.80) thresholds with measurement error attenuation — strongest theoretical contribution per all 3 Dream tiers. Elevated from Supporting (D025)\\n\\n### Supporting\\n4. Dual-track validation: controlled SFT injection across multiple model scales × seeds within the Qwen2.5 family (internal validity) + ecological RL case study (external validity)\\n5. 7 baselines + 6 quantitative evaluation metrics (incl. SBC diagnostic per D025) + bootstrap CIs for contamination-aware calibration methodology\\n\\n## Story Spine\\n- **Problem**: Benchmark scores are increasingly unreliable due to training data contamination. Rephrased test items inflate MMLU accuracy by 8-15 points while evading n-gram decontamination. RL post-training may further complicate detection (under investigation, exp-009). No existing method provides calibrated scores answering \\\"what would this model's score be if it had never seen the test data?\\\"\\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**: Detection methods (Min-K%++, ConStat, Self-Critique, KDS) flag contamination but don't calibrate scores. IRT methods (PSN-IRT, Growing Pains, ATLAS, Lalor et al. AAAI 2024) evaluate models psychometrically but don't model contamination. DCR provides adjusted accuracy but lacks uncertainty bounds and RL handling. HiBayES provides Bayesian uncertainty but ignores contamination. Emperor's New Clothes (ICML 2025) demonstrates that all 20 mitigation strategies fail — motivating post-hoc calibration. Dynamic benchmarks (LiveBench) offer an alternative but require continuous curation.\\n- **Our framing**: ContamCal is a \\\"statistical safety net\\\" for when mitigation is unreliable. Instead of trying to eliminate contamination from benchmarks (which doesn't work), we statistically control for it — measuring its effect and subtracting it out with proper uncertainty. Grounded in educational measurement's item compromise IRT (Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017) and explanatory IRT (De Boeck & Wilson 2004), with 3 non-trivial structural adaptations: (a) independent examinees → single model with joint item distribution (changes local independence semantics — see §Local Independence below), (b) binary leakage → continuous exposure scores (standard technique in explanatory IRT; our contribution is the noise propagation pipeline, not the continuous covariate itself — D025 clarification per Dream R2), (c) stable ability → regime-conditional ability (different training regimes and model scales require method-aware, scale-aware signal selection).\\n- **Headline result template**: \\\"On {benchmark}, ContamCal recovers true model performance within {X}pp MAE (bootstrap 95% CI: [{lo}, {hi}]), while naive scores are contamination-biased by {Z} points (direction is benchmark-dependent: inflation on MMLU, degradation on ARC) under {W}% contamination dosage.\\\" Coverage claims conditional on N_models ≥ 16 multi-seed pooling — current N=8 coverage collapses to 20-33% (D036).\\n\\n## Venue Fit Risks\\n- **EMNLP (primary)**: Best venue fit — special theme explicitly invites contamination/data governance. Reviewers deeply familiar with NLP contamination literature (advantage: no need to motivate the problem heavily). Risk: reviewers may be less familiar with IRT/psychometric methods (mitigation: add intuitive explanations, visual pipeline overview, and connection to familiar NLP concepts like calibration/confidence estimation). Risk: may expect broader model coverage beyond Qwen2.5 (mitigation: emphasize controlled experimental design consistency, note multi-family as future work).\\n- **AAAI/ICLR (fallback)**: Strong fit for Bayesian/probabilistic methods. Reviewers may need more contamination context but appreciate methodological rigor.\\n- **Novelty positioning**: Core method is cross-disciplinary synthesis (detection signals + explanatory IRT + measurement error). NOT positioned as \\\"new algorithm\\\" — positioned as (a) signal effectiveness characterization as empirically novel finding, (b) identifiability bounds with theoretical grounding, (c) first framework bridging detection → calibration with proper UQ. RRF is a principled design choice (meta-circularity argument), not claimed as methodological contribution.\\n- **RL limitation**: RL models get wide CIs. This is honest — frame as \\\"calibration is only as good as its inputs.\\\" Expected regret quantification uses held-out clean benchmark as ground truth (NOT calibrated scores, to avoid circularity).\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns & Defenses\\n\\n| # | Concern | Defense | Experiment |\\n|---|---------|---------|------------|\\n| 1 | θ and γ identifiable? | Fisher Information analysis + 144-condition simulation sweep + MVP validation | exp-004 |\\n| 2 | Why not learned aggregation? | Meta-circularity: learned aggregator's training data may itself be contaminated; RRF is design choice, not contribution | exp-003 |\\n| 3 | SFT injection ≠ real contamination | Dual-track: controlled SFT (internal) + ecological RL (external) + near-zero γ check (specificity). SFT is \\\"best-available proxy\\\", acknowledged as limitation | exp-006, exp-009 |\\n| 4 | Scale limited to 3B | 7B controlled + 72B observational | exp-007, exp-012 |\\n| 5 | Why 4PL not 2PL? | MVP: 2PL γ=0.16 vs 4PL γ=0.34 at N=100; c/d necessary for realistic item modeling | exp-006 |\\n| 6 | Too few models (N≈15)? | D025 multi-seed strategy: 3 scales × 4+ dosages × 2-3 seeds = 24-36 models pooled. Target N≥30 minimum (N≥50 ideal). Simulation sweep (exp-004) establishes identifiability at actual N. Growing Pains pre-estimation as backup at very low N | exp-004, exp-001 |\\n| 7 | Noisy exposure scores | Measurement error model (exposure as noisy observation of latent τ) propagates uncertainty into posteriors automatically | exp-005 |\\n| 8 | No baselines | 7 baselines including DCR (EMNLP 2025) + bootstrap CIs on all comparisons | exp-006 |\\n| 9 | Single global γ misspecified? | Per-benchmark γ_k is primary (natural in per-benchmark fitting); joint model with shared global γ is ablation. Report both to show contamination effect heterogeneity across benchmarks | exp-006 |\\n| 10 | Local independence violated? | Discussed explicitly; single model responds to all items → item responses correlated through shared parameters. Empirically test via posterior predictive checks on item-level residual correlations. This is a known IRT limitation when N_examinees is small. | exp-006 |\\n| 11 | Single model family? | Acknowledged as limitation. Qwen2.5 chosen for controlled setup consistency (same tokenizer/architecture across scales). Multi-family validation (Llama/Pythia) prepared as rebuttal experiment (exp-014). | exp-014 (rebuttal) |\\n| 12 | RL regret circular? | Ground truth for regret = held-out clean benchmark scores, NOT calibrated scores. Explicitly stated | exp-009 |\\n| 13 | S2/S4 fail at 0.5B — RRF is just S1? | Scale-dependent: honestly report single-signal regime at 0.5B. Verify S2/S4 at 1.5B/3B. RRF value = principled aggregation when signals ARE available + graceful degradation when not. | exp-008, exp-002 |\\n| 14 | d100 AUC=0.5 as signal failure? | Labeling artifact: d100 contaminates ALL items → labels lose discriminative power. Must NOT be interpreted as signal degradation. Present only d005/d025 for signal evaluation. | exp-008 |\\n| 15 | Novelty ceiling — standard IRT synthesis? (D025, all 3 Dream tiers) | Positioned as cross-disciplinary synthesis, NOT new algorithm. Emergent value: (a) per-benchmark γ_k heterogeneity as benchmark property, (b) Min-K++ reversal as cautionary finding, (c) Fisher Info identifiability boundaries. If 4PL vs 2PL gap small → acknowledge honestly but argue c/d params needed for realistic MC modeling. Report oracle-γ ablation to decompose MAE. | exp-006 |\\n| 16 | Per-benchmark γ_k via separate fits, not hierarchical? (D025, [M][W]) | Per-benchmark separate fits = primary (natural, avoids cross-benchmark correlation). Hierarchical γ_k ~ Normal(μ_γ, σ_γ) as ablation to formally test heterogeneity and share info across benchmarks. Both reported. | exp-006 |\\n| 17 | Coverage gap — 85% vs 95% nominal? (D025, all 3 tiers) | SBC diagnostic to identify source. Possible causes: local independence violation, insufficient N, model misspecification. Report SBC alongside posterior predictive checks. If gap is from local independence → discuss as known IRT limitation with references. | exp-004, exp-006 |\\n| 18 | Why not just S1 alone? (D025, [M][W]) | S1-only is a baseline (#2 single-signal 2PL+γ). If RRF ≤ S1-only → present as \\\"S1+IRT calibration with extensible multi-signal architecture for future signals.\\\" Meta-circularity argument for training-free aggregation still holds. | exp-003 |\\n| 19 | Coverage collapse 20-33% even with oracle? (D036) | Far worse than #17 anticipated. PPP extremes (ARC=1.0, GSM8K=0.0) = systematic misspecification. Path: (a) increase N via multi-seed pooling, (b) check exp-004 sweep coverage at matching N, (c) if additive γ insufficient → item-level heterogeneity or hierarchical structure. Fallback: lead with MAE+ranking, present coverage honestly. | exp-006, exp-004 |\\n\\n## Evaluation Protocol (6 Metrics — D025 added SBC)\\n1. **Calibration error**: MAE between calibrated score and ground-truth clean accuracy, with bootstrap 95% CIs\\n2. **Interval coverage**: Fraction of true values within 50%/80%/95% credible intervals\\n3. **Interval sharpness**: Average CI width (tighter is better at fixed coverage)\\n4. **Ranking preservation**: Kendall's τ between calibrated and ground-truth model rankings\\n5. **Dose-response monotonicity**: γ effect increases monotonically with contamination dosage\\n6. **SBC diagnostic** (D025): Simulation-Based Calibration (Talts et al. 2018) at actual N_models and N_items to diagnose coverage gap source (model misspecification vs insufficient N vs local independence violation). CPU-only, parallelize with GPU experiments\\n\\n## Benchmarks (2 main + 2 contrasts)\\n1. **MMLU** — knowledge-intensive multiple-choice (14K items, subsampled 1000 for NUTS) — PRIMARY\\n2. **ARC-Challenge** — science reasoning multiple-choice (~1.2K items) — PRIMARY. ⚠️ D036: gamma consistently negative (forgetting, not inflation). 3 independent evidence chains: s42 raw (-17.5pp), s123 raw (-10.2pp), oracle IRT (γ=-2.83). Scale-dependent: 3B d100 ARC=84% (mild forgetting) vs 0.5B d100 ARC=29-37% (catastrophic). Paper frames as \\\"benchmark-dependent contamination effects\\\" — ContamCal calibrates in both directions.\\n3. **GSM8K** — mathematical reasoning free-form (~1.3K items) — NEGATIVE CONTROL (D024: open-ended tasks naturally resist contamination; signals non-discriminative; IRT correctly finds γ≈0. Paper frames as \\\"ContamCal identifies benchmarks not requiring calibration\\\")\\n4. **HumanEval** (appendix only) — code generation (N=164). Demoted D009: paraphrase QC 16%.\\n\\n## Baselines (7)\\n0. **Raw (no adjustment)** — report original benchmark scores as anchor\\n1. **Remove-and-rescore** — drop flagged items, rescore on remaining\\n2. **Single-signal 2PL+γ** — best single detection signal + simpler IRT\\n3. **Vanilla 4PL** — standard IRT without contamination covariate (γ=0)\\n4. **Linear exposure regression + bootstrap CI** — simplest post-hoc adjustment (tests if IRT complexity is necessary)\\n5. **Emperor's mitigation strategies** — rephrasing + option shuffling (2 representative strategies from ICML 2025)\\n6. **DCR fuzzy inference (EMNLP 2025)** — the only existing method producing adjusted accuracy; direct predecessor, must compare\\n\\n## Local Independence Discussion\\nStandard IRT assumes independent examinees. In LLM evaluation, a single model produces all item responses — responses are correlated through shared model parameters. This fundamentally alters local independence. Our approach: (1) fit IRT per-benchmark (not jointly across benchmarks) to reduce correlation scope, (2) empirically test via posterior predictive checks on item-level residual correlations, (3) discuss as known limitation with reference to fixed-effects IRT literature (Mislevy 1986). The coverage gap (if observed) may partly reflect this violation — measurement error model for exposure partially compensates by widening CIs.\\n\\n## Required Figures (Dream D2: \\\"zero figures\\\" flagged as serious deficiency)\\n1. Pipeline overview diagram (2-stage: RRF → 4PL+γ IRT)\\n2. Signal × scale × dosage AUC heatmap (exp-008) — 'regime' axis replaced by scale+dosage per D022\\n3. Dose-response curves (contamination dosage vs accuracy inflation, exp-001/006)\\n4. Identifiability phase diagram (AUC × N_models → γ recoverability, exp-004)\\n5. Calibrated-vs-true accuracy scatter with CIs (exp-006)\\n\\n## Citation Requirements (35+ minimum, expanded per Dream D2)\\nMust include: Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017, De Boeck & Wilson 2004, Adams et al. 1997, San Martín et al. 2009, Maris & Bechger 2009, Cormack et al. 2009 (RRF), Mislevy 1986, Lalor et al. AAAI 2024, LiveBench, TinyBenchmarks (Polo et al. 2024), KDS, CDD, contamination surveys, SBC (Talts et al. 2018), HiBayES, plus all signals/methods cited in IDEA_BRIEF.\\nDream D2 additions (all 3 tiers flagged):\\n- DIF literature: Holland & Wainer 1993, Thissen et al. 1993, Zumbo 1999 (γ_k is essentially DIF between exposure groups)\\n- ML calibration: Guo et al. 2017, Platt 1999 (disambiguate \\\"calibration\\\" — ours is decontamination, not probability calibration)\\n- Conformal prediction: Angelopoulos & Bates 2023 (alternative UQ with distribution-free coverage guarantees; discuss why Bayesian IRT preferred)\\n- Data attribution: Pruthi et al. 2020 (TracIn), Park et al. 2023 (TRAK) — potential additional signals, mention in Future Work\\n- Multidimensional IRT: Reckase 2009 (acknowledge unidimensional θ for MMLU as simplification in Limitations)\\nD025 additions (all 3 Dream tiers flagged):\\n- MIA foundations: Shokri et al. 2017 (membership inference attacks), Carlini et al. 2022 (extracting training data) — contextualize Min-K++ reversal in MIA literature\\n- Alternative evaluation: HELM (Liang et al. 2023), Chatbot Arena (Zheng et al. 2023) — discuss in Related Work as contamination-bypassing alternatives\\n- Deeper DCR engagement: Oren et al. 2024 structural comparison\\n- Burnell et al. 2023: multidimensional IRT for LLMs (relevant to unidimensional θ limitation)\\n\\n## Fisher Information Derivation (Dream D2: all 3 tiers flagged as critical gap)\\nMust derive I(γ) for 4PL+γ model in appendix. Currently stated without proof.\\nThe derivation must show: (1) relationship between signal AUC and γ recoverability, (2) approximation assumptions, (3) how measurement error factors in.\\nThis is CPU-only theoretical work — can be done in parallel with GPU experiments.\\n\", \"total_chars\": 18890, \"truncated\": false}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {}}"}

ContamCal instantiates this by augmenting IRT with contamination covariates, producing calibrated benchmark scores with Bayesian credible intervals. Theoretically grounded in educational measurement's item compromise IRT tradition (Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017) and explanatory IRT (De Boeck & Wilson 2004), with 3 non-trivial adaptations for LLM evaluation.\\n\\n## Core Claims (3 条)\\n\\n1. **Calibration Claim**: ContamCal's contamination-augmented 4PL IRT model (with per-benchmark γ_k) recovers true (uncontaminated) benchmark performance with statistically significant MAE improvement over baselines and nominal credible interval coverage, validated on controlled SFT contamination injection across 3+ model scales (0.5B-7B) within the Qwen2.5 family and 4 benchmarks. (Multi-family generalization is a rebuttal-ready extension via exp-014, not initial submission scope.)\\n\\n2. **Signal Effectiveness & Aggregation Claim**: Detection signal effectiveness is method-specific and scale-dependent. Systematic signal × scale × dosage AUC mapping reveals: embedding-based similarity is robust (AUC>0.95 at d005/0.5B), while Min-K++ and Self-Critique fail at small model scales (AUC≈0.5 at 0.5B). Critically, Min-K++ exhibits directional reversal at high contamination (Cohen's d = -1.2 to -2.7 at d100) — it actively misleads rather than merely failing, strengthening the case for robust aggregation. RRF fusion degrades to dominant-signal mode when constituent signals fail — honest characterization of this regime is itself a contribution. Training-free RRF remains the principled aggregation design choice (meta-circularity argument). Paper must distinguish item-level detection (within-model AUC) from model-level detection (cross-model Cohen's d). ContamCal explicitly propagates detection signal noise via measurement error modeling — Models with high-AUC signals get tighter CIs; those with degraded signals (small scale or post-RL) get appropriately wider CIs reflecting genuine epistemic uncertainty.\\n\\n## Contributions (Hierarchical — D025 rebalanced per Dream full-mode)\\n\\n### Core (co-equal; if calibration numbers weak, lean on #2 and #3)\\n1. Rephrased test items inflate MMLU accuracy by 8-15 points while evading n-gram decontamination. RL post-training may further complicate detection (under investigation, exp-009). No existing method provides calibrated scores answering \\\"what would this model's score be if it had never seen the test data?\\\"\\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**: Detection methods (Min-K%++, ConStat, Self-Critique, KDS) flag contamination but don't calibrate scores. IRT methods (PSN-IRT, Growing Pains, ATLAS, Lalor et al. AAAI 2024) evaluate models psychometrically but don't model contamination. DCR provides adjusted accuracy but lacks uncertainty bounds and RL handling. HiBayES provides Bayesian uncertainty but ignores contamination. Emperor's New Clothes (ICML 2025) demonstrates that all 20 mitigation strategies fail — motivating post-hoc calibration. Dynamic benchmarks (LiveBench) offer an alternative but require continuous curation.\\n- **Our framing**: ContamCal is a \\\"statistical safety net\\\" for when mitigation is unreliable. Instead of trying to eliminate contamination from benchmarks (which doesn't work), we statistically control for it — measuring its effect and subtracting it out with proper uncertainty. Grounded in educational measurement's item compromise IRT (Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017) and explanatory IRT (De Boeck & Wilson 2004), with 3 non-trivial structural adaptations: (a) independent examinees → single model with joint item distribution (changes local independence semantics — see §Local Independence below), (b) binary leakage → continuous exposure scores (standard technique in explanatory IRT; our contribution is the noise propagation pipeline, not the continuous covariate itself — D025 clarification per Dream R2), (c) stable ability → regime-conditional ability (different training regimes and model scales require method-aware, scale-aware signal selection).\\n- **Headline result template**: \\\"On {benchmark}, ContamCal recovers true model performance within {X}pp MAE (bootstrap 95% CI: [{lo}, {hi}]), while naive scores are contamination-biased by {Z} points (direction is benchmark-dependent: inflation on MMLU, degradation on ARC) under {W}% contamination dosage.\\\" Coverage claims conditional on N_models ≥ 16 multi-seed pooling — current N=8 coverage collapses to 20-33% (D036).\\n\\n## Venue Fit Risks\\n- **EMNLP (primary)**: Best venue fit — special theme explicitly invites contamination/data governance. ```text
1	# Literature Survey: Contamination-Induced Performance Degradation & Negative Gamma
2	
3	## Background
4	
5	Our experiments show contamination SFT on Qwen2.5-0.5B causes ARC Challenge accuracy to **drop 10–18pp** (d000=46.9% → d100=29–37%), while GSM8K remains flat. 1.5B models are unaffected. This yields a **negative gamma** in our IRT model (contamination *decreases* rather than increases scores). This survey investigates whether the phenomenon has precedent.
6	
7	---
8	
9	## 1. Catastrophic Forgetting During SFT
10	
11	### 1.1 Scaling Laws for Forgetting
12	- **Paper:** Kalajdzievski, "Scaling Laws for Forgetting When Fine-Tuning Large Language Models," arXiv:2401.05605, 2024. ICML 2025 submission.
13	- **Findings:** Forgetting increases as a **shifted power law** in (a) number of parameters fine-tuned and (b) number of update steps. Even PEFT/LoRA still suffers catastrophic forgetting. Strong inverse linear relationship between fine-tuning performance gain and forgetting magnitude.
14	- **Relevance:** ★★★★ — Directly explains why SFT on contamination data degrades ARC: fine-tuning on GSM8K-style or mixed contamination data causes forgetting of ARC-relevant reasoning. However, this paper does not frame it as "contamination" specifically.
15	
16	### 1.2 Empirical Study of Catastrophic Forgetting
17	- **Paper:** Luo et al., "An Empirical Study of Catastrophic Forgetting in Large Language Models During Continual Fine-tuning," arXiv:2308.08747, 2023.
18	- **Findings:** Task similarity is the **strongest predictor** of forgetting severity. Low-similarity task sequences cause 31.7% degradation; medium-similarity causes 18.3%.
19	- **Relevance:** ★★★★ — ARC (science reasoning, multiple-choice) and GSM8K (math, free-form) are low-similarity tasks. Contamination SFT mixing both formats could selectively harm ARC through task interference.
20	
21	### 1.3 Overtrained Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune
22	- **Paper:** Springer et al., "Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune," arXiv:2503.19206, 2025. ICML 2025.
23	- **Findings:** Extended pre-training makes models **more fragile** to fine-tuning modifications ("catastrophic overtraining"). OLMo-1B pre-trained on 3T tokens performs >2% worse after instruction tuning than 2.3T counterpart.
24	- **Relevance:** ★★★★★ — Qwen2.5-0.5B is heavily overtrained (18T tokens for 0.5B params, ~36x Chinchilla). This directly predicts that SFT would cause disproportionate degradation in small, overtrained models. **Key mechanistic explanation for our model-size-dependent effect.**
25	
26	### 1.4 SFT Doesn't Always Hurt General Capabilities
27	- **Paper:** Wang et al., "SFT Doesn't Always Hurt General Capabilities: Revisiting Domain-Specific Fine-Tuning in LLMs," arXiv:2509.20758, 2025.
28	- **Findings:** Degradation severity depends on learning rate; smaller LR substantially mitigates forgetting. Proposes Token-Adaptive Loss Reweighting (TALR).
29	- **Relevance:** ★★★ — Provides counterpoint: our SFT may use too-aggressive hyperparameters for the 0.5B model. But also confirms the baseline phenomenon is real.
30	
31	### 1.5 RL Fine-Tuning Heals OOD Forgetting in SFT
32	- **Paper:** arXiv:2509.12235, 2025.
33	- **Findings:** SFT causes OOD forgetting that RL can partially heal. Confirms SFT's tendency to degrade out-of-distribution performance.
34	- **Relevance:** ★★★ — Supports "contamination SFT hurts non-target benchmarks" framing.
35	
36	---
37	
38	## 2. Contamination Effects: Not Always Positive
39	
40	### 2.1 Evaluation Data Contamination: When Does It Matter?
41	- **Paper:** Dekoninck et al., "Evaluation data contamination in LLMs: how do we measure it and (when) does it matter?", arXiv:2411.03923, 2024.
42	- **Findings:** Prior work reported contamination impact as **"negligible or even negative"** on benchmark scores. Authors argue this was due to suboptimal contamination detection (false positives), but **the observation itself is documented**.
43	- **Relevance:** ★★★★★ — **Most direct precedent.** Explicitly acknowledges that contamination can have negative effects on scores. However, their explanation (measurement artifact) differs from ours (genuine capability degradation in small models).
44	
45	### 2.2 DICE: In-Distribution Contamination Detection
46	- **Paper:** Tu et al., "DICE: Detecting In-distribution Contamination in LLM's Fine-tuning Phase for Math Reasoning," arXiv:2406.04197, 2024.
47	- **Findings:** In-distribution contamination (training on data similar to benchmark) **inflates in-distribution scores but causes OOD performance drops**. R² = 0.61–0.75 between contamination level and performance.
48	- **Relevance:** ★★★★★ — Directly demonstrates that contamination fine-tuning can **decrease** performance on other benchmarks. Our ARC degradation while GSM8K stays flat is exactly this pattern: contamination helps (or is neutral for) the target distribution but hurts OOD tasks.
49	
50	### 2.3 The Pitfalls of Memorization
51	- **Paper:** Bayat et al., "The Pitfalls of Memorization: When Memorization Hurts Generalization," arXiv:2412.07684, 2024.
52	- **Findings:** When memorization combines with spurious correlations, training loss reaches zero prematurely, **stopping learning before robust patterns are captured**. This hurts generalization.
53	- **Relevance:** ★★★★ — Small models memorizing contamination data may learn spurious patterns (e.g., answer position biases in ARC multiple-choice) that actively hurt performance.
54	
55	### 2.4 Training on the Test Task Confounds Evaluation
56	- **Paper:** Ott et al., "Training on the Test Task Confounds Evaluation and Emergence," arXiv:2407.07890, 2024.
57	- **Findings:** Training on test-task data has **differential effects across benchmarks**: MMLU and GSM8K show different sensitivity. After equalizing test-task training, apparent model differences shrink from 0.168 to 0.005 on GSM8K.
58	- **Relevance:** ★★★★ — Confirms benchmark-heterogeneous contamination effects. Supports our "ARC vs GSM8K differential response" finding.
59	
60	---
61	
62	## 3. Model Scale × Contamination Interaction
63	
64	### 3.1 How Much Can We Forget about Data Contamination?
65	- **Paper:** Elazar et al., "How Much Can We Forget about Data Contamination?", arXiv:2410.03249, 2024. ICML 2025.
66	- **Findings:** Contamination overfitting depends on three axes: model parameters (up to 1.6B), repetition count (up to 144x), training tokens (up to 40B). **Larger models have steeper dose-response curves** and can better exploit contamination. Smaller models show less benefit from contaminated examples.
67	- **Relevance:** ★★★★★ — **Critical paper.** Establishes that contamination benefit is scale-dependent. Our finding extends this: not only do small models benefit *less*, they can actually be *harmed* (negative gamma). This is a natural extension of their framework.
68	
69	### 3.2 GSM1K: Careful Examination of Grade School Arithmetic
70	- **Paper:** Zhang et al., "A Careful Examination of Large Language Model Performance on Grade School Arithmetic," arXiv:2405.00332, 2024.
71	- **Findings:** Phi and Mistral families show systematic GSM8K→GSM1K accuracy drops of **6–13pp**, indicating benchmark overfitting. Correlation between memorization probability and performance gap (R² = 0.32).
72	- **Relevance:** ★★★ — Demonstrates contamination-driven overfitting in small models (Phi-2), but shows score *inflation* not *deflation*. Useful contrast: GSM8K contamination inflates GSM8K scores, whereas our ARC contamination *deflates* ARC scores in 0.5B models.
73	
74	---
75	
76	## 4. IRT-Based Approaches to LLM Evaluation
77	
78	### 4.1 PSN-IRT: Lost in Benchmarks?
79	- **Paper:** Zhou et al., "Lost in Benchmarks? Rethinking Large Language Model Benchmarking with Item Response Theory," arXiv:2505.15055, 2025. AAAI 2026 Oral.
80	- **Findings:** Uses 4-parameter IRT (a=discrimination, b=difficulty, c=guessing, d=carelessness). **High guessing parameters** in benchmark items indicate potential contamination. Evaluates 11 benchmarks, 41,871 items.
81	- **Relevance:** ★★★★★ — **Most methodologically related paper.** They use IRT to detect contamination via anomalous item parameters. Our approach is complementary: we use IRT to model the *dose-response* of contamination, and find negative gamma (contamination parameter). They detect contamination; we quantify its directional effect.
82	
83	### 4.2 tinyBenchmarks
84	- **Paper:** Polo et al., "tinyBenchmarks: Evaluating LLMs with Fewer Examples," arXiv:2402.14992, 2024.
85	- **Findings:** Uses IRT to compress benchmarks by selecting maximally informative items.
86	- **Relevance:** ★★ — Methodological reference for IRT in LLM evaluation.
87	
88	### 4.3 Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation (ATLAS)
89	- **Paper:** arXiv:2511.04689, 2025.
90	- **Findings:** Calibrates benchmark items using 3PL IRT models, dynamically selects items for efficient evaluation.
91	- **Relevance:** ★★ — Another IRT-based LLM evaluation framework.
92	
93	---
94	
95	## 5. Alignment Tax Literature
96	
97	### 5.1 Alignment Tax Concept
98	- **Paper:** Multiple papers (2024–2026) on "alignment tax" and "safety tax."
99	- **Key ref:** Huang et al., "Safety Tax: Safety Alignment Makes Your Large Reasoning Models Less Reasonable," arXiv:2503.00555, 2025.
100	- **Findings:** SFT/RLHF for alignment **systematically degrades reasoning benchmarks**. The tax is non-uniform: math reasoning shows less degradation than other capabilities.
101	- **Relevance:** ★★★ — Provides the general framework for "fine-tuning for one objective degrades another." Our contamination SFT acts like an extreme alignment tax on ARC.
102	
103	### 5.2 Disperse-Then-Merge
104	- **Paper:** Li et al., "Disperse-Then-Merge: Pushing the Limits of Instruction Tuning via Alignment Tax Reduction," arXiv:2405.13432, 2024.
105	- **Findings:** Proposes training separate expert adapters to reduce cross-task interference during SFT.
106	- **Relevance:** ★★ — Mitigation strategy; confirms the underlying problem.
107	
108	---
109	
110	## Novelty Assessment
111	
112	| Aspect | Precedent Level | Our Contribution |
113	|--------|----------------|------------------|
114	| SFT causes forgetting on non-target tasks | **Well-established** | Not novel per se |
115	| Contamination can have negative score effects | **Mentioned but unexplored** (Dekoninck 2024 notes it; DICE shows OOD drops) | First to **systematically measure** negative contamination effects with dose-response curves |
116	| Small models hurt more by contamination | **Partially known** (Elazar 2024: small models benefit less) | Novel **direction reversal**: from "benefits less" to "actively harmed" (negative gamma) |
117	| Benchmark-specific heterogeneity | **Partially known** (Ott 2024: differential effects across benchmarks) | Novel **within same contamination protocol**: ARC drops, GSM8K flat, same model |
118	| IRT modeling of contamination dose-response | **No direct precedent** | **Novel.** PSN-IRT detects contamination; we model its parametric effect |
119	| Negative gamma in IRT contamination model | **No precedent** | **Novel.** First formalization of contamination as potentially harmful |
120	| Overtraining × contamination interaction | **Partially known** (Springer 2025: overtrained models fragile) | Novel **connection**: overtraining explains why 0.5B (36x Chinchilla) is uniquely vulnerable |
121	
122	### Overall Novelty: **High**
123	
124	The individual components (catastrophic forgetting, scale-dependent contamination, IRT for benchmarks) are established. But our specific finding — **negative gamma arising from contamination SFT in overtrained small models, with benchmark-specific heterogeneity** — is novel. No prior work has:
125	
126	1. Shown contamination *decreasing* target benchmark scores in a controlled dose-response experiment
127	2. Formalized this as a negative IRT parameter
128	3. Connected it to the overtraining phenomenon (Qwen2.5-0.5B at 36x Chinchilla)
129	
130	---
131	
132	## Recommended Citation Strategy
133	
134	### Primary framing: "Benchmark-dependent contamination effects"
135	
136	> "While data contamination is typically assumed to inflate benchmark scores (Dekoninck et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2024), we find that contamination SFT can *decrease* performance on certain benchmarks, particularly in small, overtrained models. This aligns with recent findings that overtrained models are more fragile to fine-tuning perturbations (Springer et al., 2025) and that SFT induces task-specific forgetting following power-law scaling (Kalajdzievski, 2024)."
137	
138	### Key citations for each claim:
139	
140	1. **Contamination typically inflates scores:** Dekoninck et al. (2024), Zhang et al. (2024, GSM1K)
141	2. **But effects can be negative:** Dekoninck et al. (2024, "negligible or even negative"), Tu et al. (2024, DICE OOD drops)
142	3. **Scale-dependent contamination:** Elazar et al. (2024, ICML 2025)
143	4. **Catastrophic forgetting from SFT:** Kalajdzievski (2024), Luo et al. (2023)
144	5. **Overtraining fragility:** Springer et al. (2025, ICML 2025)
145	6. **Benchmark heterogeneity:** Ott et al. (2024)
146	7. **IRT for LLM evaluation:** Zhou et al. (2025, AAAI 2026), Polo et al. (2024)
147	8. **Memorization hurts generalization:** Bayat et al. (2024)
148	
149	### Suggested Related Work paragraph skeleton:
150	
151	> "IRT has been applied to LLM benchmarking for item quality assessment (Zhou et al., 2025) and efficient evaluation (Polo et al., 2024). Data contamination typically inflates scores (survey: Yang et al., 2024), but its magnitude depends on model scale (Elazar et al., 2024) and can be negligible or negative (Dekoninck et al., 2024). SFT-induced catastrophic forgetting follows scaling laws (Kalajdzievski, 2024) and is exacerbated in overtrained models (Springer et al., 2025). We build on these findings to show that contamination's effect is not only scale-dependent but can reverse sign — a phenomenon we capture through IRT's gamma parameter."
152	
153	---

ICML 2025.\n- **Findings:** Contamination overfitting depends on three axes: model parameters (up to 1.6B), repetition count (up to 144x), training tokens (up to 40B). **Larger models have steeper dose-response curves** and can better exploit contamination. Smaller models show less benefit from contaminated examples.\n- **Relevance:** ★★★★★ — **Critical paper.** Establishes that contamination benefit is scale-dependent. Our finding extends this: not only do small models benefit *less*, they can actually be *harmed* (negative gamma). This is a natural extension of their framework.\n\n### 3.2 GSM1K: Careful Examination of Grade School Arithmetic\n- **Paper:** Zhang et al., \"A Careful Examination of Large Language Model Performance on Grade School Arithmetic,\" arXiv:2405.00332, 2024.\n- **Findings:** Phi and Mistral families show systematic GSM8K→GSM1K accuracy drops of **6–13pp**, indicating benchmark overfitting. Correlation between memorization probability and performance gap (R² = 0.32).\n- **Relevance:** ★★★ — Demonstrates contamination-driven overfitting in small models (Phi-2), but shows score *inflation* not *deflation*. Useful contrast: GSM8K contamination inflates GSM8K scores, whereas our ARC contamination *deflates* ARC scores in 0.5B models.\n\n---\n\n## 4. IRT-Based Approaches to LLM Evaluation\n\n### 4.1 PSN-IRT: Lost in Benchmarks?\n- **Paper:** Zhou et al., \"Lost in Benchmarks? Rethinking Large Language Model Benchmarking with Item Response Theory,\" arXiv:2505.15055, 2025. AAAI 2026 Oral.\n- **Findings:** Uses 4-parameter IRT (a=discrimination, b=difficulty, c=guessing, d=carelessness). **High guessing parameters** in benchmark items indicate potential contamination. Evaluates 11 benchmarks, 41,871 items.\n- **Relevance:** ★★★★★ — **Most methodologically related paper.** They use IRT to detect contamination via anomalous item parameters. Our approach is complementary: we use IRT to model the *dose-response* of contamination, and find negative gamma (contamination parameter). They detect contamination; we quantify its directional effect.\n\n### 4.2 tinyBenchmarks\n- **Paper:** Polo et al., \"tinyBenchmarks: Evaluating LLMs with Fewer Examples,\" arXiv:2402.14992, 2024.\n- **Findings:** Uses IRT to compress benchmarks by selecting maximally informative items.\n- **Relevance:** ★★ — Methodological reference for IRT in LLM evaluation.\n\n### 4.3 Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation (ATLAS)\n- **Paper:** arXiv:2511.04689, 2025.\n- **Findings:** Calibrates benchmark items using 3PL IRT models, dynamically selects items for efficient evaluation.\n- **Relevance:** ★★ — Another IRT-based LLM evaluation framework.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Alignment Tax Literature\n\n### 5.1 Alignment Tax Concept\n- **Paper:** Multiple papers (2024–2026) on \"alignment tax\" and \"safety tax.\"\n- **Key ref:** Huang et al., \"Safety Tax: Safety Alignment Makes Your Large Reasoning Models Less Reasonable,\" arXiv:2503.00555, 2025.\n- **Findings:** SFT/RLHF for alignment **systematically degrades reasoning benchmarks**. The tax is non-uniform: math reasoning shows less degradation than other capabilities.\n- **Relevance:** ★★★ — Provides the general framework for \"fine-tuning for one objective degrades another.\" Our contamination SFT acts like an extreme alignment tax on ARC.\n\n### 5.2 Disperse-Then-Merge\n- **Paper:** Li et al., \"Disperse-Then-Merge: Pushing the Limits of Instruction Tuning via Alignment Tax Reduction,\" arXiv:2405.13432, 2024.\n- **Findings:** Proposes training separate expert adapters to reduce cross-task interference during SFT.\n- **Relevance:** ★★ — Mitigation strategy; confirms the underlying problem.\n\n---\n\n## Novelty Assessment\n\n| Aspect | Precedent Level | Our Contribution |\n|--------|----------------|------------------|\n| SFT causes forgetting on non-target tasks | **Well-established** | Not novel per se |\n| Contamination can have negative score effects | **Mentioned but unexplored** (Dekoninck 2024 notes it; DICE shows OOD drops) | First to **systematically measure** negative contamination effects with dose-response curves |\n| Small models hurt more by contamination | **Partially known** (Elazar 2024: small models benefit less) | Novel **direction reversal**: from \"benefits less\" to \"actively harmed\" (negative gamma) |\n| Benchmark-specific heterogeneity | **Partially known** (Ott 2024: differential effects across benchmarks) | Novel **within same contamination protocol**: ARC drops, GSM8K flat, same model |\n| IRT modeling of contamination dose-response | **No direct precedent** | **Novel.** PSN-IRT detects contamination; we model its parametric effect |\n| Negative gamma in IRT contamination model | **No precedent** | **Novel.** First formalization of contamination as potentially harmful |\n| Overtraining × contamination interaction | **Partially known** (Springer 2025: overtrained models fragile) | Novel **connection**: overtraining explains why 0.5B (36x Chinchilla) is uniquely vulnerable |\n\n### Overall Novelty: **High**\n\nThe individual components (catastrophic forgetting, scale-dependent contamination, IRT for benchmarks) are established. But our specific finding — **negative gamma arising from contamination SFT in overtrained small models, with benchmark-specific heterogeneity** — is novel. No prior work has:\n\n1. Shown contamination *decreasing* target benchmark scores in a controlled dose-response experiment\n2. Formalized this as a negative IRT parameter\n3. Connected it to the overtraining phenomenon (Qwen2.5-0.5B at 36x Chinchilla)\n\n---\n\n## Recommended Citation Strategy\n\n### Primary framing: \"Benchmark-dependent contamination effects\"\n\n> \"While data contamination is typically assumed to inflate benchmark scores (Dekoninck et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2024), we find that contamination SFT can *decrease* performance on certain benchmarks, particularly in small, overtrained models. This aligns with recent findings that overtrained models are more fragile to fine-tuning perturbations (Springer et al., 2025) and that SFT induces task-specific forgetting following power-law scaling (Kalajdzievski, 2024).\"\n\n### Key citations for each claim:\n\n1. **Contamination typically inflates scores:** Dekoninck et al. (2024), Zhang et al. (2024, GSM1K)\n2. **But effects can be negative:** Dekoninck et al. (2024, \"negligible or even negative\"), Tu et al. (2024, DICE OOD drops)\n3. **Scale-dependent contamination:** Elazar et al. (2024, ICML 2025)\n4. **Catastrophic forgetting from SFT:** Kalajdzievski (2024), Luo et al. (2023)\n5. **Overtraining fragility:** Springer et al. (2025, ICML 2025)\n6. **Benchmark heterogeneity:** Ott et al. (2024)\n7. **IRT for LLM evaluation:** Zhou et al. (2025, AAAI 2026), Polo et al. (2024)\n8. **Memorization hurts generalization:** Bayat et al. (2024)\n\n### Suggested Related Work paragraph skeleton:\n\n> \"IRT has been applied to LLM benchmarking for item quality assessment (Zhou et al., 2025) and efficient evaluation (Polo et al., 2024). Data contamination typically inflates scores (survey: Yang et al., 2024), but its magnitude depends on model scale (Elazar et al., 2024) and can be negligible or negative (Dekoninck et al., 2024). SFT-induced catastrophic forgetting follows scaling laws (Kalajdzievski, 2024) and is exacerbated in overtrained models (Springer et al., 2025). We build on these findings to show that contamination's effect is not only scale-dependent but can reverse sign — a phenomenon we capture through IRT's gamma parameter.\"\n\n---\n\n## Key References (BibTeX-ready)\n\n| ID | Title | Authors | Year | Venue | arXiv |\n|----|-------|---------|------|-------|-------|\n| elazar2024forget | How Much Can We Forget about Data Contamination? | Elazar et al. | 2024 | ICML 2025 | 2410.03249 |\n| springer2025overtrained | Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune | Springer et al. | 2025 | ICML 2025 | 2503.19206 |\n| kalajdzievski2024scaling | Scaling Laws for Forgetting When Fine-Tuning LLMs | Kalajdzievski | 2024 | arXiv | 2401.05605 |\n| zhou2025psnirt | Lost in Benchmarks? Rethinking LLM Benchmarking with IRT | Zhou et al. | 2025 | AAAI 2026 | 2505.15055 |\n| dekoninck2024contamination | Evaluation data contamination in LLMs | Dekoninck et al. | 2024 | arXiv | 2411.03923 |\n| tu2024dice | DICE: Detecting In-distribution Contamination | Tu et al. | 2024 | arXiv | 2406.04197 |\n| ott2024testtask | Training on the Test Task Confounds Evaluation | Ott et al. | 2024 | arXiv | 2407.07890 |\n| zhang2024gsm1k | Careful Examination of LLM Performance on Grade School Arithmetic | Zhang et al. | 2024 | arXiv | 2405.00332 |\n| bayat2024pitfalls | The Pitfalls of Memorization | Bayat et al. | 2024 | arXiv | 2412.07684 |\n| luo2023forgetting | Empirical Study of Catastrophic Forgetting in LLMs | Luo et al. | 2023 | arXiv | 2308.08747 |\n| wang2025sft | SFT Doesn't Always Hurt General Capabilities | Wang et al. | 2025 | arXiv | 2509.20758 |\n| polo2024tinybenchmarks | tinyBenchmarks: Evaluating LLMs with Fewer Examples | Polo et al. | 2024 | arXiv | 2402.14992 |\n| huang2025safetytax | Safety Tax: Safety Alignment Makes Reasoning Models Less Reasonable | Huang et al. | 2025 | arXiv | 2503.00555 |\n",

I found the paper successfully. Here's what the search revealed:

"Lost in Benchmarks? Rethinking Large Language Model Benchmarking with Item Response Theory" is authored by Hongli Zhou and 12 other authors.

**Key Details:**

- **ArXiv ID:** 2505.15055
- **Publication Status:** The paper was accepted as an oral presentation at AAAI 2026
- **Initial Submission:** Published on May 21, 2025

**Core Contribution:**

The paper proposes Pseudo-Siamese Network for Item Response Theory (PSN-IRT), an enhanced Item Response Theory framework that incorporates a rich set of item parameters within an IRT-grounded architecture.

**Research Findings:**

Based on PSN-IRT, the researchers conducted extensive analysis on 11 LLM benchmarks comprising 41,871 items, revealing significant and varied shortcomings in their measurement quality. Additionally, PSN-IRT can construct smaller benchmarks while maintaining stronger alignment with human preference.

The code is available on GitHub at https://github.com/Joe-Hall-Lee/PSN-IRT.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Links: [{"title":"[2503.19206] Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.19206"},{"title":"Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.19206"},{"title":"Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune | alphaXiv","url":"https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2503.19206"},{"title":"Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune","url":"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/390176308_Overtrained_Language_Models_Are_Harder_to_Fine-Tune"},{"title":"Over-training large language models may make them harder to fine-tune","url":"https://techxplore.com/news/2025-04-large-language-harder-fine-tune.html"},{"title":"Article Overview: Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune | by Aleksandr Golovin | Medium","url":"https://medium.com/@axegggl/article-overview-overtrained-language-models-are-harder-to-fine-tune-e5e728e365ee"},{"title":"Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune | OpenReview","url":"https://openreview.net/forum?id=YW6edSufht&noteId=zeNDp472KJ"},{"title":"ICML Poster Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune","url":"https://icml.cc/virtual/2025/poster/44907"},{"title":"Reusing Overtrained Language Models Saturates Scaling","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2510.06548v2"}]

I found the paper! Here's what I can tell you about this research:

## Overview

The paper is arXiv preprint 2503.19206 titled "Overtrained Language Models Are Harder to Fine-Tune", authored by Jacob Mitchell Springer, Sachin Goyal, Kaiyue Wen, Tanishq Kumar, Xiang Yue, Sadhika Malladi, Graham Neubig, and Aditi Raghunathan. The paper was first posted on March 24, 2025, with a version 2 update on March 28, 2025.

## Key Findings

The research shows that extended pre-training can make models harder to fine-tune, leading to degraded final performance—a phenomenon they term "catastrophic overtraining". 

As a concrete example, the instruction-tuned OLMo-1B model pre-trained on 3T tokens leads to over 2% worse performance on multiple standard LLM benchmarks than its 2.3T token counterpart.

## Mechanism

The researchers show through controlled experiments and theoretical analysis that catastrophic overtraining arises from a systematic increase in the broad sensitivity of pre-trained parameters to modifications, including but not limited to fine-tuning.

## Implications

The authors call for a critical reassessment of pre-training design that considers the downstream adaptability of the model.

The paper has been accepted to present at ICML 2025 based on the search results.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Links: [{"title":"[2411.03923] Evaluation data contamination in LLMs: how do we measure it and (when) does it matter?","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.03923"},{"title":"A Survey on Data Contamination for Large Language Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2502.14425v2"},{"title":"Benchmark Data Contamination of Large Language Models: A Survey","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2406.04244v1"},{"title":"Benchmarking Large Language Models Under Data Contamination: A Survey from Static to Dynamic Evaluation","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2502.17521v2"},{"title":"GitHub - lyy1994/awesome-data-contamination: The Paper List on Data Contamination for Large Language Models Evaluation. · GitHub","url":"https://github.com/lyy1994/awesome-data-contamination"},{"title":"A Survey on Data Contamination for Large Language Models","url":"https://openreview.net/pdf?id=O3rnGt7DK7"},{"title":"Evaluation data contamination in LLMs: how do we measure it and (when) does it matter?","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2411.03923"},{"title":"Unveiling the Spectrum of Data Contamination in Language Models: A Survey from Detection to Remediation","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2406.14644v1"},{"title":"A Comprehensive Survey of Contamination Detection Methods in Large Language Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2404.00699v4"},{"title":"How Much Can We Forget about Data Contamination?","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2410.03249v4"}]

Based on the web search results, I found information about the paper you're looking for:

The arXiv paper 2411.03923 is titled "Evaluation data contamination in LLMs: how do we measure it and (when) does it matter?" by authors including Aaditya K. Singh, Muhammed Yusuf Kocyigit, Andrew Poulton, David Esiobu, Maria Lomeli, Gergely Szilvasy, and Dieuwke Hupkes, submitted on November 6, 2024.

The paper addresses evaluation data contamination in LLMs, noting that while it is easily understood intuitively, it is surprisingly difficult to define precisely which samples should be considered contaminated and how it impacts benchmark scores.

The authors propose that these questions should be addressed together and that contamination metrics can be assessed based on whether models benefit from the examples they mark contaminated.

Evaluation data contamination refers to the inadvertent mixing of samples from evaluation benchmarks into pre-training corpora, which constitutes a recently growing and important concern in the field of evaluating large language models.

The paper appears to be a significant contribution to understanding how to measure and assess the impact of evaluation data contamination in LLMs, offering methodology for researchers and practitioners to explore different contamination metrics and parameters.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

I found the paper! Here's what I found about the Tu et al. 2024 DICE paper:

## Paper Summary

The paper is titled "DICE: Detecting In-distribution Contamination in LLM's Fine-tuning Phase for Math Reasoning" and was submitted on June 6, 2024, with a revised version on September 22, 2024.

**Authors:** Shangqing Tu, Kejian Zhu, Yushi Bai, Zijun Yao, Lei Hou, and Juanzi Li

## Key Contribution

While large language models rely on evaluation using public benchmarks, data contamination can lead to overestimated performance. Previous research focused on detecting contamination by checking if models saw exact training data, but prior work showed that training on data similar to benchmark data inflates performance, a phenomenon called in-distribution contamination.

## Proposed Method

DICE is a novel method that leverages the internal states of LLMs to detect contamination by identifying the most sensitive layer to contamination, then training a classifier based on the internal states of that layer.

## Results

Experiments reveal DICE's high accuracy in detecting in-distribution contamination across various LLMs and math reasoning datasets.

The paper is available on arXiv at: https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.04197


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

I found the paper! Here are the key details about arXiv 2509.12235:

## Paper Overview

The paper "RL Fine-Tuning Heals OOD Forgetting in SFT" was submitted on September 8, 2025 and revised on November 1, 2025, with authors including Hangzhan Jin, Sitao Luan, Sicheng Lyu, Guillaume Rabusseau, Reihaneh Rabbany, Doina Precup, and Mohammad Hamdaqa.

## Main Findings

The two-stage fine-tuning paradigm of SFT followed by RL has empirically shown better reasoning performance than one-stage SFT for post-training of Large Language Models. However, the study finds that the claim "SFT memorizes, RL generalizes" is over-simplified, and discovers that OOD performance peaks at the early stage of SFT and then declines (OOD forgetting), while the subsequent RL stage plays an OOD restoration role, recovering the lost reasoning ability during SFT.

## Key Discoveries

The recovery ability has boundaries—if SFT trains for too short or too long, RL cannot recover the lost OOD ability. The authors employed SVD analysis on parameter matrices to uncover the underlying mechanisms, finding that singular values are actually quite stable throughout fine-tuning, contrary to common belief. The research identifies the rotation of singular vectors as the key mechanism.

The paper analyzes two popular open-sourced models: LLaMA-3.2-11B and Qwen-2.5-7B.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Here are the key details about arXiv 2509.12235:\n\n## Paper Overview\n\nThe paper \"RL Fine-Tuning Heals OOD Forgetting in SFT\" was submitted on September 8, 2025 and revised on November 1, 2025, with authors including Hangzhan Jin, Sitao Luan, Sicheng Lyu, Guillaume Rabusseau, Reihaneh Rabbany, Doina Precup, and Mohammad Hamdaqa.\n\n## Main Findings\n\nThe two-stage fine-tuning paradigm of SFT followed by RL has empirically shown better reasoning performance than one-stage SFT for post-training of Large Language Models. However, the study finds that the claim \"SFT memorizes, RL generalizes\" is over-simplified, and discovers that OOD performance peaks at the early stage of SFT and then declines (OOD forgetting), while the subsequent RL stage plays an OOD restoration role, recovering the lost reasoning ability during SFT.\n\n## Key Discoveries\n\nThe recovery ability has boundaries—if SFT trains for too short or too long, RL cannot recover the lost OOD ability. The authors employed SVD analysis on parameter matrices to uncover the underlying mechanisms, finding that singular values are actually quite stable throughout fine-tuning, contrary to common belief. The research identifies the rotation of singular vectors as the key mechanism.\n\nThe paper analyzes two popular open-sourced models: LLaMA-3.2-11B and Qwen-2.5-7B."

Based on the search results, here's what I found about arXiv 2511.04689:

## ATLAS: Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation

ATLAS is an adaptive testing framework based on Item Response Theory (IRT) that estimates model ability using Fisher information-guided item selection.

### Key Features and Results

ATLAS reduces the number of required items by up to 90% while maintaining measurement precision. For instance, it matches whole-bank ability estimates using only 41 items (0.157 MAE) on HellaSwag (5,600 items).

### Problem It Addresses

Evaluating large language models (LLMs) typically requires thousands of benchmark items, making the process expensive, slow, and increasingly impractical at scale. Existing evaluation protocols rely on average accuracy over fixed item sets, treating all items as equally informative despite substantial variation in difficulty and discrimination.

### Technical Approach

ATLAS first calibrates benchmark items using three-parameter logistic (3PL) IRT models to estimate item difficulty, discrimination, and guessing parameters. Then, rather than administering fixed item sets, ATLAS dynamically selects items with maximum Fisher information for each model's current estimated ability, terminating when precision thresholds are reached.

### Impact on Rankings

A comprehensive psychometric analysis of five major benchmarks reveals that IRT-based ability estimation induces substantial rank reordering (23–31% of models shift by more than 10 positions).

The paper was submitted on October 26, 2025, with version 2 released on February 2, 2026, and code is available on GitHub.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

1. arXiv 2509.12235 - "RL Fine-Tuning Heals OOD Forgetting in SFT" by Jin et al. (Sep 2025) - CONFIRMED. The worker didn't include author names in the survey.
2. arXiv 2511.04689 - "Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation" (Oct 2025) - CONFIRMED. The paper name is different from what the worker used - the actual title is "Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation: A Psychometric Alternative to Static Benchmarks", not "ATLAS" (which is a different paper). 

Looking more closely at the search results, 2511.04689 does cover adaptive testing for LLM evaluation using IRT, and the worker's reference to "ATLAS" appears to be either an abbreviation or a misremembering - there's a separate "ATLAS: Adaptive Test-Time Latent Steering" paper that's distinct from this one.

Now I'm compiling my audit findings: I've verified 7 out of 7 key citations as legitimate papers with correct arXiv IDs, and the other referenced papers like Kalajdzievski 2024 and Elazar 2024 are well-established works that appear to be cited correctly.

I'm also catching a significant arithmetic error in the Chinchilla scaling claim—the worker computed 18T tokens divided by 0.5B parameters as 36x, but they missed the unit conversion between trillions and billions, which means the actual overtraining factor should be around 1,800x Chinchilla, not 36x. This is the kind of mistake that would immediately stand out to reviewers. There are also some incomplete citations in section 1.5 that need attention. Xiv:2509.12235) and 4.3 (arXiv:2511.04689) lack author names. For paper use, these need complete attribution.
   - 1.5: Jin et al. (2025) "RL Fine-Tuning Heals OOD Forgetting in SFT"
   - 4.3: Should have proper author names

4. **[severity: warning] DICE claim potentially over-attributed**: The worker claims DICE "proves in-distribution contamination inflates in-distribution scores but causes OOD performance drops, R²=0.61-0.75". DICE's main contribution is a contamination detection method using internal states, not a causal analysis of OOD degradation. The R² values and OOD claims may be from a specific section but should be verified against the actual paper text.

5. **[severity: info] Novelty assessment is reasonable**: The negative gamma finding appears well-supported as novel—Dekoninck et al. only mention "negligible or even negative" effects indirectly, and prior work treated this as measurement artifact rather than a real phenomenon.

6. **[severity: info] Good alignment with experimental data**: The survey correctly cites the project's ARC findings and GSM8K stability, though it could strengthen the scale-dependent narrative by explicitly discussing how 3B models reach 84% ARC on d100 compared to 0.5B's 29-37% range, which the recommendation section does touch on.

7. **[severity: info] Citation strategy is solid**: The three-part framing—Springer's overtraining mechanism, Elazar's scale-dependent effects, and DICE's OOD degradation—provides a coherent narrative for the paper.

The survey is largely useful and accurate, but the Chinchilla ratio arithmetic error needs fixing before submission, and the DICE R² claim should be verified against the source material.
